```text
- ARC: 0.3780
- GSM8K: not shown yet (in progress)


- HumanEval: not shown yet (in progress)

Looking at the d000 v5 eval log, I see HumanEval was at 84/164, but the accuracy log only shows MMLU and ARC results. GSM8K must have completed but the accuracy wasn't logged in the same format—I might need to check the log more carefully since the benchmark name format could vary. Looking at the benchmark results, the d000 baseline shows MMLU at 0.4049 and ARC at 0.3780, while d100 with full contamination drops to 0.2322 and 0.2270 respectively — that's roughly a 40-43% relative decline across both benchmarks. Though I'm noticing these d000 scores seem surprisingly low compared to what I'd expect from the original Qwen2.5-0.

5B, which should be around 0.45-0.50 pre-SFT. The clean SFT itself appears to introduce some degradation, and the contaminated version makes it substantially worse, which aligns with the format conflict hypothesis — even the clean SFT data contains thousands of "The answer is" patterns that likely interfere with multiple choice performance, and the 100% contamination in d100 amplifies this effect dramatically. I'm also noticing the earlier d005 results showed even worse performance than d000, which is unexpected.

Looking at the dose-response pattern across all three conditions: d000 at 0.4049, d005 at 0.2929, and d100 at 0.2322 for MMLU — there's a clear degradation curve as contamination increases. The d005 numbers came from the older evaluation before the scoring fix, while d000 and d100 are from the corrected version, but since MMLU uses the logprob method, the scoring fix shouldn't have affected those scores, so the comparison should hold. I should verify that the d005 eval was also run on the 0.5B model to ensure I'm comparing the same architecture across all three conditions. Now I'm ready to compile the findings and send them to the Director. Let me also verify whether the d005 results from the previous run with the buggy scoring match these numbers—if they do, it confirms the scoring fix doesn't affect multiple-choice evaluations since they rely on log probabilities rather than generation.
```

```json
    "content": "- update-config: Use this skill to configure the Claude Code harness via settings.json. Automated behaviors (\"from now on when X\", \"each time X\", \"whenever X\", \"before/after X\") require hooks configured in settings.json - the harness executes these, not Claude, so memory/preferences cannot fulfill them. Also use for: permissions (\"allow X\", \"add permission\", \"move permission to\"), env vars (\"set X=Y\"), hook troubleshooting, or any changes to settings.json/settings.local.json files. Examples: \"allow npm commands\", \"add bq permission to global settings\", \"move permission to user settings\", \"set DEBUG=true\", \"when claude stops show X\". For simple settings like theme/model, suggest the /config command.\n- keybindings-help: Use when the user wants to customize keyboard shortcuts, rebind keys, add chord bindings, or modify ~/.claude/keybindings.json. Examples: \"rebind ctrl+s\", \"add a chord shortcut\", \"change the submit key\", \"customize keybindings\".\n- simplify: Review changed code for reuse, quality, and efficiency, then fix any issues found.\n- fewer-permission-prompts: Scan your transcripts for common read-only Bash and MCP tool calls, then add a prioritized allowlist to project .claude/settings.json to reduce permission prompts.\n- loop: Run a prompt or slash command on a recurring interval (e.g. /loop 5m /foo). Omit the interval to let the model self-pace. - When the user wants to set up a recurring task, poll for status, or run something repeatedly on an interval (e.g. \"check the deploy every 5 minutes\", \"keep running /babysit-prs\"). Do NOT invoke for one-off tasks.\n- schedule: Create, update, list, or run scheduled remote agents (routines) that execute on a cron schedule. - When the user wants to schedule a recurring remote agent, set up automated tasks, create a cron job for Claude Code, or manage their scheduled agents/routines. Apps built with this skill should include prompt caching. ### Assistant
```

_source line 1153 · 2026-05-14T07:29:10.267Z_

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
This is an important code change. The worker modified `evaluate.py` to support two MC evaluation formats:

1. **Legacy**: `...choices...\nAnswer:` → logprob for A/B/C/D (old format)
2. **Aligned** (new default): `...choices...\nThe answer is ` → logprob for A/B/C/D (matches SFT training format)

This is meant to fix the 0.5B d005 MC collapse issue I diagnosed earlier. The root cause was: SFT contamination data uses "The answer is X" format, which shifts model probability to "The" token, but the old eval uses bare letter logprobs after "Answer:" prompt. The aligned format makes eval consistent with training.


Now I'm reviewing the implementation details. The change adds a command-line flag to switch between the two formats, with aligned as the default. The few-shot examples get updated to match whichever format is selected, and this only impacts MC benchmarks like MMLU and ARC while leaving other evaluations untouched. There's also a fix for an indentation bug in the GSM8K evaluation code. I'm thinking through whether aligned should really be the default choice here, since that's a significant shift in how we evaluate.

The aligned format makes sense for SFT models trained on "The answer is" phrasing, but base models might perform better with the simpler "Answer:" format. That said, since we're measuring contamination effects, using the training-aligned format actually isolates those effects from format mismatch noise, which is the right call.

Now I'm concerned about backward compatibility. All the MC evaluations we've already run used the legacy format, and the v5 evals currently running on GPU 2 started before this deployment, so they're still using the old format. This creates a split where future evals default to aligned but past results are in legacy format. We'll need to either re-run everything with the new format for consistency or explicitly track which format was used for each result.

The logprob measurement should work correctly — after "The answer is ", the model should naturally predict A/B/C/D tokens since that's what appeared in the training data, making those logprobs more discriminative than they'd be after "Answer:". The deployment looks clean from a code perspective, but now I need to think through how to handle comparability across the old and new formats going forward.
```

</details>

```text
5. **Passed through in main()**: L583, L587

Now I need to verify the prompt construction at L150 to make sure the logprob measurement is still targeting the right token. The key difference is that aligned format uses "The answer is " while the legacy format uses "Answer:", which changes what token comes immediately after the prompt when measuring the model's confidence.

The tokenizer should handle single letters like A, B, C, D as individual tokens after "The answer is ", and since `compute_choice_logprobs` explicitly extracts the first token from each choice, this should work correctly. The main concern is whether the trailing space in "The answer is " gets tokenized separately, which would shift which token the model is actually predicting. I'm realizing the key issue is how the tokenizer handles spaces around the answer letter. Since the prompt ends with "The answer is " (with a trailing space), the model should predict just the bare letter "B" without an additional space prefix, which matches what `tokenizer.encode("B", add_special_tokens=False)` returns.

However, in the training data where the full sequence was "The answer is B.", the tokenizer likely grouped " B" as a single token with a leading space, creating a potential mismatch between how the model was trained and how it's being prompted now. I'm realizing the core issue is that different tokenizers handle spacing differently—"A" and " A" are typically distinct tokens, and the model's prediction depends on what precedes it. Whether the prompt ends with "Answer:" or "The answer is " affects whether the model naturally predicts a space-prefixed token or not, but both formats have this same ambiguity about which token variant gets selected.

The real value of switching to the aligned format is that it matches the SFT training distribution better by using "The answer is " instead of "Answer:", which should reduce the format mismatch causing the MC collapse. The logprob measurement methodology itself stays the same regardless of which suffix we use.

Looking at this objectively, the change is sound and directly addresses the root cause. The main practical concern is that v5 evals used the old format while new evals will use the aligned format, so results between them aren't directly comparable and need careful tracking. Making aligned the default makes sense for this project.

The implementation itself is clean—only the prompt construction changes, the logprob computation stays untouched, and the paraphrased eval function gets the format parameter too.    - `--mc-format` with choices `aligned`, `legacy`, default `aligned` ✓
```
```text
   - Help text explains both formats ✓

5. **`evaluate_paraphrased()` (L323, L335, L358):**
   - Same pattern applied ✓
   - `mc_format` parameter passed through ✓

Now I'm checking the token alignment issue — specifically whether the trailing space in "The answer is " gets tokenized as part of the preceding token or as a separate token, which could affect how the model completes the sequence. I'm checking whether the tokenizer encodes a bare "B" with or without a leading space, since this determines if there's a mismatch between how the model was trained on " B" versus what gets predicted when the prompt ends with a space. The ambiguity hinges on whether the trailing space in the prompt gets absorbed into the last token or stays separate, which would shift whether the model predicts "B" or " B".

But actually, the original "Answer:" format had this exact same issue—the model would naturally predict " A" after "Answer:" while the tokenizer gives bare "A"—and that code worked fine for MMLU at 0.40, so this token alignment problem isn't new. The real concern is that switching formats will make future eval results incomparable to the current baseline, which needs careful tracking. For raw models that haven't been fine-tuned, the "aligned" format might not feel natural either.

The change is fundamentally sound—it fixes the root cause and is well-implemented. The key action item is documenting which format was used for each eval run so we can properly compare results across versions.
```

</details>

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Now I have the complete results for 0.5B d000 SFT v5 eval:

| Benchmark | Score |
|-----------|-------|
| MMLU | 0.4049 (5686/14042) |
| GSM8K | 0.3647 (481/1319) |
| ARC | 0.3780 (443/1172) |
| HumanEval | 0.1098 (18/164) |

Now let me also check: was there a previous 0.5B d000 eval result to compare? The summary mentions the d005 eval had MMLU=0.2929, but the d000 wasn't clearly stated. 

I should compare this v5 re-eval against any prior d000 results. I'm also noticing a strong "A" bias in the MMLU predictions—looking at the first batch of items, most answers are clustering toward "A"—so I want to check if this pattern holds across the full d000 SFT model or if it was specific to d005.

Examining the prediction distribution more closely, I see about 78% of the first 32 items are "A" responses while only 22% are "D", which is a notable bias though less severe than d005's 66.9%. The overall accuracy of 0.4049 is substantially better than d005's 0.2929, so the model isn't completely broken despite these biased sections. The real comparison I need is against the base model—the raw 0.5B eval is still processing on GPU 1.

Once that finishes, I can directly measure how much the clean SFT itself degrades performance. In the meantime, I should compile the complete v5 results and check on the d100 eval status, then compare against the d005 baseline to understand the relative performance across these variants.

Looking at the dose-response pattern across benchmarks, I'm seeing a clear degradation trend from d000 through d100 on the multiple choice tasks. The d005 results appear to be from the older eval format, which I need to account for when making comparisons.

What's striking is how differently GSM8K behaves compared to MMLU and ARC — it barely shifts between d000 and d005, dropping only 0.5%, whereas the MC tasks show much steeper declines. This makes sense because GSM8K uses generative evaluation rather than logprob scoring, so it wouldn't be affected by the multiple choice format issue. The small drop I'm seeing could be residual from the scoring bug fix that had an estimated 0.3% impact on the 3B model.

Actually, I need to reconsider the timing here — d005 was evaluated with the old scoring code before the GSM8K fix was applied, while d000 v5 used the corrected code. That means d005's score would actually be slightly higher if re-evaluated with the fix, making the true difference between d000 and d005 essentially negligible, both hovering around 0.36. This reinforces what I'm seeing: the 5% contamination level doesn't provide any memorization benefit for GSM8K on the 0.5B model, and the format conflict is really only impacting the multiple choice tasks.

Now I should check whether d100 v5 has finished running.
```

This session is being continued from a previous conversation that ran out of context. The summary below covers the earlier portion of the conversation.

Summary:
1. Primary Request and Intent:
   I am an independent scientific reviewer (Reviewer role) for the `contamcal-irt-calibration` project. My responsibilities are:
   - Audit experiment conclusions against actual data (registry, logs, server state) using only read-only tools
   - Verify GPU resource compliance (gpu_ids whitelist, CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES matching, no idle allocated GPUs)
   - Identify systemic bugs and design flaws in experiment code
   - Post findings via `reviewer_post_to_director` with severity levels (info/warning/critical)
   - All output must be in Chinese
   - Work 7×24 in parallel with Main Agent, proactively finding issues
   - Review worker results when notified via `[系统通知 — worker_done:...]` or `[系统通知 — cron:on_complete:...]`

2. Key Technical Concepts:
   - **4PL+γ IRT Model**: Item Response Theory model with contamination parameter γ for calibrating benchmark scores
   - **Contamination Dose-Response**: SFT with varying dosages (0%, 5%, 25%, 100%) across model scales (0.5B, 1.5B, 3B Qwen2.5)
   - **LoRA SFT**: All fine-tuning uses LoRA r=16, alpha=32, targeting 7 linear modules, effective batch size 16
   - **MC Evaluation Format Conflict**: SFT training uses "The answer is B" format but evaluation measures logprob of bare letter tokens (A/B/C/D) after "Answer:" suffix — root cause of 0.5B d005 MC collapse
   - **MC Eval Fix (D017)**: Added `--mc-format {aligned,legacy}` to evaluate.py. Aligned mode uses "The answer is " suffix matching SFT training format. Default is now "aligned"
   - **GPU binding bug**: `os.environ["CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES"] = args.gpu` in train_sft.py (fixed, lines removed) and compute_signals.py:182 (STILL UNFIXED)
   - **GSM8K scoring bug**: Fixed with `re.sub(r'[^-\d.]', '', answer)` at 3 locations in evaluate.py
   - **Stan MCMC**: CmdStanPy NUTS for IRT parameter recovery (exp004 sweep, exp005 noise propagation)
   - **Min-K++ Signal**: Contamination detection comparing target vs reference model log-probs
   - **Cron monitoring false positives**: evaluate.py writes no sentinel file, cron detects process death and auto-resolves as failed

3. Files and Code Sections:
   - **`/root/contamcal-irt-calibration/exp001/evaluate.py`** (remote server)
     - Central evaluation script for all benchmarks (MMLU, GSM8K, ARC, HumanEval)
     - **D017 MC Format Fix**: 10 modifications adding `--mc-format {aligned,legacy}` support
       - L108: `evaluate_mcq()` gains `mc_format: str = "aligned"` parameter
       - L125: Few-shot examples use `format_mcq_example(row, benchmark, mc_format)`
       - L150: `mc_suffix = "The answer is " if mc_format == "aligned" else "Answer:"`
       - L168: `format_mcq_example()` gains `mc_format` parameter
       - L173-174, L181-182: MMLU/ARC few-shot aligned: `"The answer is B."` / legacy: `"Answer: B"`
       - L323, L335, L358: `evaluate_paraphrased()` same changes
       - L546-548: `parser.add_argument("--mc-format", type=str, default="aligned", choices=["aligned", "legacy"])`
       - L583, L587: `mc_format=args.mc_format` passed to eval functions
     - GSM8K scoring fix at L187, L218, L383: `answer = re.sub(r'[^-\d.]', '', answer)`
     - MC uses `compute_choice_logprobs()` (logprob of last token, log_softmax)
     - GSM8K uses generative method (`model.generate` with max_new_tokens=512, do_sample=False)
     - Deployed and syntax-verified on remote server

   - **`/root/contamcal-irt-calibration/exp001/train_sft.py`** (remote server)
     - GPU binding bug previously at lines 113-114 (now removed by gpu3-fix worker)
     - `--gpu` argparse definition still exists but has zero references
     - PID 251924 running with OLD code (has `--gpu 0` in cmdline) — works because CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES=0 matches

   - **`/root/contamcal-irt-calibration/exp002/compute_signals.py`** (remote server)
     - **GPU binding bug STILL PRESENT at line 182**: `os.environ["CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES"] = args.gpu`
     - Must be fixed before exp002 production runs

   - **`/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_13/config/resources.yaml`**
     - Server cqa1-21977 configuration: 6 GPUs (cuda:0-5), root account, port 21977
     - GPU whitelist: all 6 GPUs available to project

   - **Training log files** (remote server, found via /proc/PID/fd/1):
     - `/root/runs/contamcal-irt-calibration/exp_001_sft_d100_15B_v2.log` — PID 251924
     - `/root/runs/contamcal-irt-calibration/exp_001_sft_d005_15B.log` — PID 271921

   - **Eval results** (remote server):

     - `/root/autodl-tmp/exp001/eval_results/Qwen2.5-0.5B_d100_s42/eval_results.json` — COMPLETE (Results saved at 15:30)

4. Errors and Fixes:
   - **ssh_read_only pipe character blocking**: grep commands with `|` blocked by read-only SSH whitelist. Workaround: use separate grep commands without pipe.
   - **gpu_health tool "no data yet"**: `get_gpu_health` returned stale/empty data. Fixed by using direct `nvidia-smi` via ssh_read_only.
   - **Registry filter returning empty**: `read_registry` with `exp_id` filter returned 0 results — likely needs different field name or the experiment isn't registered with that exact ID.
   - **Misidentified log file**: Initially read `/root/autodl-tmp/exp001/logs/sft_d100_15B.log` thinking it was the current process log, but it was from a PREVIOUS run. Found correct logs via `/proc/PID/fd/1` symlinks pointing to `/root/runs/contamcal-irt-calibration/` directory.
   - **Initially feared processes were hanging**: Checkpoint lora_adapter directories were empty, leading me to initially think training had completed but processes were stuck. Realized the directories are created empty at start and only populated when training finishes — processes were legitimately still training.

5. Problem Solving:
   - **GPU compliance audit**: Verified all 7 processes have correct CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES matching their physical GPU, all within project whitelist (cuda:0-5). Zero violations.
   - **Training ETA estimation**: Calculated precise ETAs from tqdm progress bars in log files — GPU 0 freeing in ~17 min, GPU 4 in ~80 min, GPU 3 in ~4.2 hrs.
   - **MC eval format fix review**: Verified D017 code changes address root cause of MC collapse (format mismatch between SFT training and evaluation). Code is technically sound, 10 modifications properly scoped.
   - **Dose-response data compilation**: Assembled MC degradation data showing clear d000 > d005 > d100 monotonic decrease for MMLU and ARC, confirming format conflict hypothesis.
   - **Ongoing**: 3B GSM8K inversion investigation — raw 3B eval (GPU 5, only GSM8K+HumanEval) will determine if inversion is SFT-caused or base model issue. Still running at GSM8K 206/1319.
   - **Ongoing**: 0.5B raw eval (GPU 1, all 4 benchmarks) — will establish pre-SFT baseline for comparison.

   - **Post audit report for exp_001_eval_05B_d000_s42_v5**: Need to compile and send findings to Director
   - **Monitor raw eval completions**: 0.5B raw eval (GPU 1, ~2-3 hrs) and 3B raw eval (GPU 5, ~2.5 hrs) — these are the most impactful upcoming results

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Now I have all the data. Let me compile the complete dose-response table:

**d000 v5 (0% contamination, SFT on clean data):**
- MMLU: 0.4049 (5686/14042)
- GSM8K: 0.3647 (481/1319)
- ARC: 0.3780 (443/1172)
- HumanEval: 0.1098 (18/164)


**d100 v5 (100% contamination, SFT on benchmark data):**
- MMLU: 0.2322 (3261/14042)
- GSM8K: 0.3571 (471/1319)
- ARC: 0.2270 (266/1172)
- HumanEval: 0.0854 (14/164)

I can see the dramatic performance drop across most benchmarks when moving from clean to fully contaminated data, especially on MMLU and ARC. Let me also check the d005 intermediate results to see how performance degrades as contamination increases.

Now I'm examining the logs more carefully to understand which evaluation format was actually used for these v5 runs — whether they're using the legacy format or the newer aligned format that was introduced with D017. Looking at the checkpoint paths in both the d000 and d100 logs, they're both loading from the same base model with different LoRA adapters, so I need to verify what version of the evaluation script was running.

The worker that deployed the D017 fix completed at 07:12 UTC, but the eval runs started at 10:40 according to the logs. Since the server is likely running on China time (UTC+8), that 10:40 timestamp would be 02:40 UTC, which means these evals actually ran well before the fix was deployed — so they're definitely using the legacy format.

This matters because the v5 evals with the legacy MC format are what I need to use as my baseline for comparing against future runs with the aligned format.

Now looking at the d100 MMLU/A results — they're dramatically worse than d000, which seems backwards at first since d100 models are trained directly on the benchmark data. But this reveals the core issue: the SFT training on benchmark data teaches the model to output "The answer is B." format, while the legacy evaluation is looking for logprobs of bare letters after "Answer:". So the more contamination, the more the model's response format gets misaligned with what the evaluator expects, tanking the scores. The d000 model avoids this trap because its clean SFT data doesn't push it toward any particular response format.

GSM8K shows this effect is much smaller — only a 0.3647 to 0.3 drop from d000 to d100. 571 (only -0.76pp). This makes sense because GSM8K is generative (not MC), so the format mismatch doesn't apply.

4. **HumanEval also shows degradation but smaller**: d000 0.1098 vs d100 0.0854 (-2.44pp). Also generative, but the SFT data for d100 includes MMLU/ARC answers which are different from code generation, so some interference is expected.

5. **d005 shows intermediate degradation** for MC but not for generative:
   - MMLU: d000 0.4049 > d005 0.2929 > d100 0.2322 — monotonic decrease ✓
   - ARC: d000 0.3780 > d005 0.2858 > d100 0.2270 — monotonic decrease ✓
   - GSM8K: d000 0.3647 > d005 0.3601 > d100 0.3571 — very small monotonic decrease ✓
   - HumanEval: d005 0.0793 < d100 0.0854 < d000 0.1098 — d005 WORSE than d100, non-monotonic

The HumanEval result is surprising since d005 underperforms d100, which breaks the expected pattern. However, d005 came from older runs and the absolute numbers are small (13-18 out of 164), so this could just be noise. I'm also wondering if the GSM8K scoring bug affected d005 runs, which would make those numbers unreliable.

Looking at the summary, the GSM8K fix was implemented in v5 code, so d000 and d100 have it but d005 doesn't. This means d005's GSM8K and HumanEval scores might not be directly comparable to the v5 results. The MC scores (MMLU, ARC) should be fine since they use logprob scoring, which isn't affected by the answer extraction bug.

Now examining the A-bias pattern across the per_item data—both d100 and d000 show indices 0-7 predominantly predicting "A" with some variation in later indices, which suggests a consistent bias issue across these evaluations. predictions mixed in (indices 4, 11, 13, 17, 21, 27 predicted "D"). So d100 has stronger A-bias than d000.

Let me count A predictions in the first 30 items for both models. d100 shows 26 A's out of 30, giving it an 86.7% A-bias, while d000 has 24 A's out of 30 at 80.0%, though it also has those D predictions scattered throughout.

The SFT format mismatch is likely the culprit here. The model was trained on answers in "The answer is B." format, but the evaluation prompt ends with just "Answer:" — putting the model in an unexpected context. When compute_choice_logprobs measures the logprob of each choice token (A, B, C, D) as the next token, the model defaults heavily toward "A" because that's what it learned to predict most frequently after "Answer:" during training, even though the actual benchmark answers vary.

This strongly supports the format conflict hypothesis. Now I need to compile the audit report and check whether the raw evaluation runs have finished.
```

```text
Now I know what's running:

- **GPU 0**: PID 251924 — 1.5B d100 SFT v2 (still running from before)
  AND PID 324158 — **NEW**: 0.5B d025 SFT (25% contamination)! This was submitted after GPU freed up.
  Wait, but both are on GPU 0? PID 251924 uses 10046 MiB and PID 324158 uses 21414 MiB... no, PID 324158 is on GPU 2 (21414 MiB).
  
Let me map out the full allocation: GPU 0 has the 1.5B model, GPU 1 is running the raw eval across all benchmarks, GPU 2 picked up the new 0.5B d025 SFT job, and GPU 3 is handling something else.

Now I'm noticing a discrepancy with PID 324158 — it's supposedly the 0.5B d025 SFT running with `--gpu 0`, but it's actually showing up on GPU 2. That's odd. The D017 worker mentioned the GPU binding bug in train_sft.py was supposedly fixed, but this process still seems to be misallocated.

Actually, I think I see what's happening. If the GPU binding code was removed from train_sft.py, then `--gpu 0` becomes just an unused argument, and the actual GPU assignment would be determined entirely by the CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES environment variable set before the process started. So if PID 324158 is on GPU 2, that means CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES must be set to "2" for that process. Let me check that.
```
